They transport and distribute water via widely branched networks from sources to consumers. In order to guarantee a working network at all times, the water supply company continuously monitors the network and takes actions when necessary – e.g. reacting to leakages, sensor faults and drops in water quality.\nL44: Since real world networks are too large and complex to be monitored by a human, algorithmic monitoring systems have been developed. A popular type of such systems are residual based anomaly detection systems that can detect events such as leakages and sensor faults. For a continuous high quality monitoring, it is necessary for these systems to adapt to changed demands and presence of various anomalies.\nL45: In this work, we propose an adaption of the incremental SAM-kNN classifier for regression to build a residual based anomaly detection system for water distribution networks that is able to adapt to any kind of change.\nL46: \nL47: J. Jakob and A. Repository files navigation\nL199: \nL200:   *   * cite83†README L201:   * cite83†License L202: \nL203: More items\nL204: # SAM-kNN Regressor for Online Learning in Water Distribution Networks\nL205: \nL206: This repository contains the implementation of the methods proposed in the paper cite82†SAM-kNN Regressor for Online Learning in Water Distribution Networks by Jonathan Jakob, André Artelt, Martina Hasenjäger and Barbara Hammer.\nL207: \nL208: The experiments as described in the paper are implemented in the folder cite84†Implementation .\nL209: ## Abstract\nL210: Water distribution networks are a key component of modern infrastructure for housing and industry. They transport and distribute water via widely branched networks from sources to consumers. In order to guarantee a working network at all times, the water supply company continuously monitors the network and takes actions when necessary -- e.g. reacting to leakages, sensor faults and drops in water quality.\nL211: Since real world networks are too large and complex to be monitored by a human, algorithmic monitoring systems have been developed. A popular type of such systems are residual based anomaly detection systems that can detect events such as leakages and sensor faults. For a continuous high quality monitoring, it is necessary for these systems to adapt to changed demands and presence of various anomalies.\nL212: In this work, we propose an adaption of the incremental SAM-kNN classifier for regression to build a residual based anomaly detection system for water distribution networks that is able to adapt to any kind of change.\nL213: ## Details\nL214: \nL215: ### Implementaton of experiments\nL216: \nL217: The shell script `run_experiments.sh` runs all experiments\nL218: \nL219: ### Other (important) stuff\nL220: \nL221: #### `SAM_KNN_Regression.py`\nL222: \nL223: Implementation of our proposed SAM-kNN regressor.\nL224: \nL225: ## Data\nL226: \nL227: Note that we did not publish the data sets due to large file sizes. Please contact us if you are interested in the data sets.\nL228: \nL229: ## Requirements\nL230: \nL231:   * Python3.6\nL232:   * Packages as listed in `Implementation/REQUIREMENTS.txt`\nL233: \nL234: ## License\nL235: \nL236: MIT license - See cite80†LICENSE L237: ## About\nL238: \nL239: No description, website, or topics provided.\nL240: \nL241: ### Resources\nL242: \nL243: cite71†Readme L244: \nL245: cite72†License L246: \nL247: cite73†Activity L248: \nL249: ### Stars\nL250: \nL251: 0 stars\nL252: \nL253: ### Watchers\nL254: \nL255: 0 watching\nL256: \nL257: ### Forks\nL258: \nL259: cite74†0 forks L260: \nL261: cite75†Report repository L262: \nL263: ## Releases\nL264: \nL265: ## Packages\nL266: \nL267: ## Contributors\nL268: \nL269: ## Languages\nL270: \nL271: You can’t perform that action at this time.\n"

42-## 1. Introduction
43-
44-As urban populations and city sizes continue to grow, urban water distribution systems (WDSs), as a fundamental component for ensuring public welfare, are facing increasing complexity and management challenges [10,25]. To maintain the stability of WDSs, accurate forecasting and proactive preparation for future demands are essential [11,35]. The predictive lead time establishes critical temporal buffers for municipal water operators, strengthening their operational resilience. However, short-term demand forecasting in real-life WDSs proves challenging due to the need for predictions at all nodes based on limited sensor layouts and complex network topology. The stochastic variability of water usage, driven by human activities, complicates the forecasting process [26]. Given the hurdles in distribution reliability and the capricious nature of water consumption, accurate real-time demand forecasting is crucial for ensuring a reliable and resilient WDS [12].
45-
46:Generally, to estimate the water usage at non-monitored sites, a well-calibrated hydraulic model is typically required for indirect observation [4,5,9,15]. The hydraulic model, with their robust theoretical basis and comprehensible parameter settings, serves as the foundation and primary source of information for water network management [8,27,36], and is widely understood and trusted by practitioners. Hydraulic simulation was conducted to enhance the estimation credibility of a real-world network, and demand forecasting can then be obtained by time series forecasting algorithms [23]. However, the uncertainties associated with the water demand and the condition of infrastructure assets make constructing and maintaining a well-calibrated WDS highly complex and time-consuming [1,33]. Achieving real-time efficient forecasting based on the hydraulic model seems unattainable, especially for cities lacking a hydraulic model. Their research demonstrated the robustness of the GNN model to various levels of noise. By applying the gated graph neural network (GGNN), Li et al. [18] were the first to achieve the state forecasting for water quality. The GGNN algorithm, derived from gated recurrent unit (GRU), was applied for the first time in the domain of the WDS, yielding promising spatiotemporal predictive outputs. Although the aforementioned methods can be used for predicting future water demand states, they still have limitations. The methods in the first two studies were limited to estimating the current state. While integrating time series prediction algorithms with their methods could achieve future state forecasting, the simple stacking of time series forecasting and state estimation would lead to error accumulation. The GGNN method offers a novel approach to developing alternative models for state forecasting in WDSs. However, directly applying the GGNN method does not tightly integrate with the characteristics of water networks. Additionally, GGNN requires recurrent graph convolution operations to expand the receptive field, which limits its application in large-scale pipe networks and leads to over-smoothing issues.
47-
48-Despite excellent results and a broad range of applications, GNN models are still treated as black boxes [16]: the underlying mechanisms that steer the learning process and generalization ability remain a challenging and open question [29]. Merely achieving good performance is not enough to guarantee the deployment of models in production [13,21], especially in critical operational contexts where additional performance metrics such as robustness or safety are paramount. In such environments, human operators must comprehend and trust the model's predictions. Also, a deeper understanding of the model would improve its predictive performance [22]. Therefore, the second goal of this paper is to explain the results generated by the model.
49-
50-The literature [34] defines a classification standard for interpretability methods within the GNN framework. These methods are categorized into model-level and instance-level explanations. The former elucidates the behavior and decision-making process of the entire model, while the latter provides input-specific explanations for each graph instance. Model-based explanations offer a macroscopic understanding of the overall model behavior by identifying general patterns and rules under different input scenarios. Within this context, the concept of explainability is tied to specific model fragments. These explanations delve into the inference-making process of the model, providing a comprehensive account of which features significantly impact the inference results [14,28]. These techniques analyze the inference process to determine which features significantly influence outputs, enabling retrospective examination of parameter contributions. Model-level explanations can be further divided into three parts: rule extraction, hidden semantics analysis, and attribution methods [20].

75-## 3. Experimental setups
76-
77:### 3.1. Dataset
78-
79-Real-world WDSs typically lack comprehensive nodal monitoring, preventing direct validation of demand forecasts at non-instrumented nodes. To address this limitation and assess model performance under realistic conditions, this study adopt the benchmark l-Town Network dataset [30]. As depicted in Fig. 2(a), this network has substantial elevation variations, with a maximum difference of 74.2 m, and is serviced by two water tanks. It includes notable high and low areas where water pressure is regulated by a pressurized pumping station and a pressure-reducing valve, respectively. This network essentially covers the characteristics found in typical real WDSs.

87-## 4. Results and discussion
88-
89-This section presents the results from both forecasting performance and model explainability analyses. First, this study report and analyze the forecasting task results: the proposed method achieves superior spatial generalization and temporal forecasting accuracy compared to previous approaches. Subsequently, this study evaluates the robustness of the AGN model across varying prediction windows. Finally, once demonstrated the quality of our predictions, this study explains the recurrent layers, attention mechanisms and nodal features used in the model, demonstrating how these components influence the final output of the model.

227-
228:Haze pollution, driven by PM_{2.5}, poses significant risks to public health and environmental sustainability, particularly in urban-industrial regions like the Fenwei Plain, China. This study introduces the Lasso-Cox Dynamic Attention Network (LCDAN), a novel multimodal framework for modeling PM_{2.5} transmission risks. By integrating Generalized Autoregressive Conditional Heteroskedasticity (GARCH (1,1)) for volatility analysis, graph neural networks (GNNs) with attention mechanisms for spatial path representation, extended Long Short-Term Memory (xLSTM) for temporal dynamics, and SHAP for interpretability, LCDAN captures the complex spatio-temporal evolution of PM_{2.5} propagation networks. Using a comprehensive dataset (2019–2023) encompassing air pollutants, meteorological, socio-economic, and geographic data, the model outperforms eight benchmarks, achieving a 5.5% average F1 score improvement and up to 38% for low-risk scenarios. A case study in Xi’an (November–December 2023) demonstrates LCDAN’s ability to identify critical transmission pathways and risk nodes, with SHAP analysis highlighting industrial emissions and economic density as key drivers. The model’s efficiency (42-minute training, <15 ms online inference) and interpretability make it a robust tool for air quality management, offering actionable insights for policymakers to mitigate haze pollution effectively.

## Results in Engineering

Volume 27, September 2025, 106911

# 

Research paper

Explainable graph neural network for real-time demand states forecasting in water distribution system

https://doi.org/10.1016/j.rineng.2025.106911Get rights and content

Under a Creative Commons license

Open access

## Highlights

  * â€¢

Attention-enhanced GNN for real-time demand forecasting based on pressure data.

  * â€¢

Model is independent from hydraulic model, offering broader applicability.

  * â€¢

Model exhibits high spatiotemporal accuracy and sustained long-term effectiveness.

  * â€¢

Model explanation reveals its operational mechanisms, facilitating field deployment.

## Abstract

Efficient real-time nodal demand forecasting in water distribution systems is essential for a reliable and resilient water supply. Given the efficiency of the data-driven approach and the intricate node-line structure of water networks, graph-based machine learning methods offer a promising approach. However, the scarcity of real-time flow acquisition devices limits the ability to directly forecast timely demand. Pressure sensors provide a more practical alternative. Thus, this study proposes a novel attention-augmented gated graph neural network (AGN) for real-time demand forecasting from a pressure perspective. By integrating attention mechanisms, the AGN overcomes limitations of convolution-based graph neural networks, such as capturing long-range dependencies and dynamic node interactions, leading to enhanced performance on real-world modeling problems. This model is formulated for real and sensitive contexts where, in addition to being accurate, the predictions must also be understandable by human operators. Thus, model-level explanations are then introduced in our explainable framework. The proposed AGN outperforms benchmark methods in both spatiotemporal accuracy and long-term forecasting effectiveness, while simultaneously delivering interpretable insights into the demand forecasting process. The novel approach based on the fusion of forecasting and explainability in a single framework enables the creation of powerful and reliable systems suitable for real-world issues and challenges.

## 1. Introduction

As urban populations and city sizes continue to grow, urban water distribution systems (WDSs), as a fundamental component for ensuring public welfare, are facing increasing complexity and management challenges [10,25]. To maintain the stability of WDSs, accurate forecasting and proactive preparation for future demands are essential [11,35]. The predictive lead time establishes critical temporal buffers for municipal water operators, strengthening their operational resilience. However, short-term demand forecasting in real-life WDSs proves challenging due to the need for predictions at all nodes based on limited sensor layouts and complex network topology. The stochastic variability of water usage, driven by human activities, complicates the forecasting process [26]. Given the hurdles in distribution reliability and the capricious nature of water consumption, accurate real-time demand forecasting is crucial for ensuring a reliable and resilient WDS [12].

Generally, to estimate the water usage at non-monitored sites, a well-calibrated hydraulic model is typically required for indirect observation [4,5,9,15]. The hydraulic model, with their robust theoretical basis and comprehensible parameter settings, serves as the foundation and primary source of information for water network management [8,27,36], and is widely understood and trusted by practitioners. Hydraulic simulation was conducted to enhance the estimation credibility of a real-world network, and demand forecasting can then be obtained by time series forecasting algorithms [23]. However, the uncertainties associated with the water demand and the condition of infrastructure assets make constructing and maintaining a well-calibrated WDS highly complex and time-consuming [1,33]. Achieving real-time efficient forecasting based on the hydraulic model seems unattainable, especially for cities lacking a hydraulic model. Consequently, the primary objective of this study focuses on developing an innovative state estimation framework that operates independently of conventional hydraulic modeling paradigms.

Several studies [18,33,37] have enabled direct modeling of WDSs, obviating the need for hydraulic models. Based on the assumption of smooth variations in nodal head (the sum of nodal pressure and elevation of a node), Zhou et al. [37] developed a graph-based head reconstruction (GHR) method. This method decomposes the hydraulic head into components with different variation speeds, reconstructing the head using the slowly varying components obtained from a limited number of monitoring nodes. However, this method heavily relies on having a sufficient number of well-defined sensor nodes and a regular underlying graph structure. Xing and Sela [33] were the pioneers in employing a graph neural network (GNN) to achieve state estimation within WDSs. They developed an encoder-decoder GNN architecture, enabling real-time synchronous estimation of pipeline flow and nodal pressure states. Their research demonstrated the robustness of the GNN model to various levels of noise. By applying the gated graph neural network (GGNN), Li et al. [18] were the first to achieve the state forecasting for water quality. The GGNN algorithm, derived from gated recurrent unit (GRU), was applied for the first time in the domain of the WDS, yielding promising spatiotemporal predictive outputs. Although the aforementioned methods can be used for predicting future water demand states, they still have limitations. The methods in the first two studies were limited to estimating the current state. While integrating time series prediction algorithms with their methods could achieve future state forecasting, the simple stacking of time series forecasting and state estimation would lead to error accumulation. The GGNN method offers a novel approach to developing alternative models for state forecasting in WDSs. However, directly applying the GGNN method does not tightly integrate with the characteristics of water networks. Additionally, GGNN requires recurrent graph convolution operations to expand the receptive field, which limits its application in large-scale pipe networks and leads to over-smoothing issues.

Despite excellent results and a broad range of applications, GNN models are still treated as black boxes [16]: the underlying mechanisms that steer the learning process and generalization ability remain a challenging and open question [29]. Merely achieving good performance is not enough to guarantee the deployment of models in production [13,21], especially in critical operational contexts where additional performance metrics such as robustness or safety are paramount. In such environments, human operators must comprehend and trust the model's predictions. Also, a deeper understanding of the model would improve its predictive performance [22]. Therefore, the second goal of this paper is to explain the results generated by the model.

The literature [34] defines a classification standard for interpretability methods within the GNN framework. These methods are categorized into model-level and instance-level explanations. The former elucidates the behavior and decision-making process of the entire model, while the latter provides input-specific explanations for each graph instance. Model-based explanations offer a macroscopic understanding of the overall model behavior by identifying general patterns and rules under different input scenarios. Within this context, the concept of explainability is tied to specific model fragments. These explanations delve into the inference-making process of the model, providing a comprehensive account of which features significantly impact the inference results [14,28]. These techniques analyze the inference process to determine which features significantly influence outputs, enabling retrospective examination of parameter contributions. Model-level explanations can be further divided into three parts: rule extraction, hidden semantics analysis, and attribution methods [20].

This study innovative proposes an explainable attention-augmented gated graph neural network (AGN) framework for water demand states forecasting from a pressure perspective. Derived from GRU-based GNN (i.e. GGNN), this model not only exhibits excellent time series prediction performance but also captures the networkâ€™s structure. The innovative attention mechanisms are then incorporated to address the limitation of GGNN in capturing solely local dependencies and enhance the model's interpretability through its transparent decision-making process. Overall, the paper encompasses the following novelties:

Â·Demand forecasting model designed for water network characteristics: A core innovation of this study is the development of the AGN model, specifically designed to capture the unique spatio-temporal characteristics of water distribution networks. The proposed AGN model forecasts future demands at all nodes, without relying on hydraulic models. The AGN model shows significant accuracy in both spatial and temporal dimensions compared to existing direct modeling methods. The model maintains high predictive robustness across various forecasting horizons. Our high accuracy results validate the feasibility and efficiency of demand prediction from a pressure perspective.

Model explanation associated with water network characteristics: Merely achieving sufficient prediction accuracy is not enough to guarantee the modelâ€™s deployment in real-world WDSs. To bridge this gap, the second innovation addresses the crucial need for improved model transparency. Specifically, the key lies in integrating model explanations with practical engineering issues, enabling network managers to understand and trust the model, thereby making reliable decisions. To the best knowledge, we are the first to address interpretability issues in WDS. By providing these explanations, this study provided a deeper understanding of the roles of different model components in the prediction task. This process also aids in improving the model by offering feedback to further enhance its predictive performance.

## 2. Methodology

This study proposes an Attentive Graph Network (AGN) to forecast water demand in distribution systems using pressure data without hydraulic modeling. The architecture leverages the inherent graph structure of water networks, historical pressure measurements, and nodal features to predict future nodal demands. Specifically designed to capture unique pressure characteristics in water distribution systems (WDS), the model includes built-in interpretability mechanisms to ensure transparency. The following sections detail the challenges addressed and present the explainable AGN framework.

### 2.1. Problem formulation

Before discussing the issues and challenges, this study provide a detailed introduction to the symbolic representation of WDS as a graph. The WDS is represented as an undirected graph ð’¢=(ð’±,â„°,ð’²). Here, ð’± is the set of ð¼+ð¼* vertices representing nodes that comprise reservoirs, tanks and junctions. â„° is the set of edges representing the valves, pumps, and pipes. ### 2.2. Explainable forecasting framework

The GRU [6] is a highly optimized variant of the recurrent neural network. Its core advantage lies in its unique architectural design. Through the precise regulatory mechanisms of the update gate and reset gate, the GRU skillfully balances the retention and forgetting of historical information [7]. This allows it to capture the dynamics of long-term and short-term dependencies in time series data [24]. The GGNN, an innovative extension of the GRU [17], integrates recurrent layers, graph convolution, and gating mechanisms. It retains the advantages of the GRU in time series prediction while deeply analyzing the complex structure of non-Euclidean space graph data through graph convolution operations, achieving efficient utilization of graph data [31]. However, the GGNN also faces limitations of the convolution-based graph methods: Each iteration only focuses on the first-order neighborhood of the sensor nodes. Thus, the iteration of the recurrent layers restricts the generalization of sensor node features to a larger network space [32].
(4) Integration of nodal type and height features: By calculating the external-attention of the CHPSs to the nodal height and nodal type features, the potential nodal features can be obtained. These features are then fused with the CHPSs using addition and multiplication, respectively, to forecast the future nodal heads:(10)â„Ž(ð‘™)ð‘£ð‘–,ð‘¡ð‘—=(ð‘(ð‘™)ð‘£ð‘–,ð‘¡ð‘—+Ìƒð¹â¡â„Žð‘£ð‘–)âŠ™Ìƒð¹â¡ð‘¡ð‘£ð‘– where potential height feature Ìƒð¹â¡â„Žð‘£ð‘– and the potential type feature Ìƒð¹â¡ð‘¡ð‘£ð‘– are obtained by calculating the external-attention of CHPS ð‘(ð‘™)ð‘£ð‘–,ð‘¡ð‘— to nodal height ð¹â¡â„Žð‘£ð‘– (Eq. ### 2.3. Cascade masking operation

Prior research [18] demonstrates that masking operations improve model generalization in water network applications. The core strategy involves randomly selecting a proportion of sensor nodesâ€™ input during training. These inputs are then set to zero, simulating prediction scenarios for non-monitored sites. This approach effectively enhances the modelâ€™s ability to generalize to indirectly observed nodes, making it an effective method for data augmentation.

Building on this concept, this study introduces the cascade masking operation as a novel enhancement strategy. Renowned for its multi-level refinement in complex image processing tasks [2,3], such as instance segmentation, cascade masking can progressively enhance both segmentation accuracy and robustness. This study adapts this advanced concept to graph processing by incrementally decreasing the number of randomly masked sensor measurements and their features during training. This staged approach systematically strengthens the model's ability to capture complex interdependencies between sensor data and network topology, significantly improving generalization to unmonitored nodes.

## 4. Results and discussion

This section presents the results from both forecasting performance and model explainability analyses. First, this study report and analyze the forecasting task results: the proposed method achieves superior spatial generalization and temporal forecasting accuracy compared to previous approaches. Subsequently, this study evaluates the robustness of the AGN model across varying prediction windows. Finally, once demonstrated the quality of our predictions, this study explains the recurrent layers, attention mechanisms and nodal features used in the model, demonstrating how these components influence the final output of the model.

### 4.1. Forecasting results

#### 4.1.1. Spatial generalization accuracy

This study aims to utilize the GNN algorithm to predict the water demand states from a pressure perspective. A thorough review of previous research revealed that, although several pioneering studies have explored the estimation or forecasting of WDS states without relying on pre-built hydraulic models, they still face limitations. Notably, 82.8 % of total nodes have an MAE below 0.5 m, a widely recognized threshold for practical application in hydraulic model calibration. Compared to competing models, AGN shows a significant advantage in the proportion of nodes below this threshold, outperforming GGNN and GHR+GRU methods by 79 % and 73.4 %, respectively, highlighting its superior spatial generalization performance.

Further analysis of Fig. 3(b) reveals a profound correlation between prediction error and nodal head variation. Nodes with errors between 0.5 m and 1.0 m are mainly concentrated in high-elevation areas, while low-elevation nodes generally face challenges with MAE greater than 2.0 m. This phenomenon indicates an imbalance in model training, particularly as low-elevation nodes are not sufficiently learned due to sparse sensor placement. We attribute this primarily to two factors: (1) sensor scarcity in low-elevation regions hinders the model's ability to capture localized hydraulic characteristics, and (2) insufficient model capacity to discern elevation-driven hydraulic variations. These findings underscore the critical impact of sensor deployment density on data-driven model performance, highlighting the need for comprehensive data acquisition to optimize model training.

Image: Fig 3

  1. Download: Download high-res image (1MB)
  2. Download: Download full-size image

Fig. 3. Comparison of forecasting models in spatial direction: (a) The real nodal heads variation; (b) The MAE loss of AGN model; (c) The MAE loss of GGNN model; (d) The MAE loss of GHR+GRU method.

Admittedly, in the face of complex water network components, drastic elevation changes, and sparse sensor deployment, the AGN model still faces considerable challenges in pursuing comprehensive and accurate predictions. However, it is noteworthy that even under such extreme conditions, the AGN model demonstrates remarkable predictive ability in the high-elevation area, despite the sensors in this area still being relatively sparse â€” 100 % of high-elevation nodes have an MAE below 1.0 m. In the context of low-density sensor deployment, the AGN can still effectively address sparse sensor environments, reducing reliance on sensing equipment. More real-world WDSs will benefit from our sensor-robust model.

As shown in Fig. 3(b), the AGN model achieves satisfactory prediction accuracy and breadth in the medium-elevation area. This suggests that in water distribution systems (WDSs) characterized by relatively gentle terrain and moderate head changes, the AGN model can deliver enhanced predictive performance. These results not only further consolidate the practicality and reliability of the AGN model but also lays a solid theoretical and practical foundation for its widespread application in complex WDS management, heralding a promising future for data-driven technology in intelligent WDS management.

Due to their relatively low deployment and maintenance costs, pressure sensors have been widely used in WDSs. This provides a rich data source for the AGN model and practically verifies the applicability and feasibility of water demand prediction from pressure data. This practice-oriented research perspective offers new ideas and methodological support for WDS management.


Further comparative analysis reveals that, unlike AGN and GGNN which also incorporate GRU components, the GHR+GRU method exhibits significant instability in its time-series performance, with markedly increased fluctuation amplitudes. This phenomenon may indicate the limitations of the GHR method in handling water networks with extreme head variations and suboptimal sensor placement. It emphasizes the spatial generalization challenges of accurately predicting non-sensor nodesâ€™ state based on limited sensor measurements. And this is the key challenge in data-driven water network modeling.

Although this study achieves high-precision predictions with MAPE not exceeding 5 % over time, it seems unacceptable when faced with a wide head variation range of 41.1 m to 113.6 m (Fig. 3(a)). This is mainly due to the approximately 4 % of low-elevation nodes, whose MAPE reaches as high as 80 %, significantly raising the overall average MAPE. The error levels for different elevation regions are analyzed in detail in the next section.
This is primarily due to the attention mechanismâ€™s ability to capture long-distance correlations, allowing nodes to transcend physical distance limitations. Through the self-attention mechanism, the model can learn and transfer the pressure characteristics from sensor nodes to all nodes, rather than being limited to the sensor nodesâ€™ neighborhood. Therefore, spatial generalizing process can be summarized as two significant mechanisms: neighbor topology feature transferring based on graph convolution, and global feature generalization based on the self-attention mechanism. Based on these two key mechanisms, the AGN model can generalize the learned graph structure-related features to all nodes in WDS, achieving state forecasting.

In the GGNN, ensuring sensor nodes can â€œperceiveâ€ the farthest non-sensor nodes theoretically requires up to 11 steps of recurrent iterations. However, this approach presents significant practical challenges, particularly due to the substantial increase in training time. For example, training with 11 iterations of recurrent layers on an A10 tensor core GPU in the small-scale network studied here takes about two days. This duration is unacceptable for practical applications in large-scale WDSs, where non-sensor nodes are further away from their nearest sensor node. In contrast, using 3 recurrent layers reduces the training time to about 3 h, demonstrating significant efficiency advantages.

Further analysis reveals that increasing the number of recurrent layers from 1 to 2 significantly reduces the MAPE. However, increasing beyond 3 layers leads to a rise in MAPE. This suggests that too many recurrent layers may introduce excessive non-sensor node features, causing data redundancy or pollution, and weakening the modelâ€™s prediction performance. Additionally, regardless of the number of recurrent layers, the cumulative maximum MAE remains stable at 96.03 %, revealing an optimization bottleneck in specific regions, such as low-elevation region in this study. These regions, due to insufficient sensor placement, cannot improve training results by increasing the number of recurrent layers.

In summary, incorporating the self-attention mechanism in the recurrent layers significantly enhances the modelâ€™s prediction accuracy. Intuitively, sensor nodes in central areas (e.g., 458, 549, and 752) enjoy higher weights due to their critical positions, significantly enhancing the overall prediction performance. In contrast, sensors at the network periphery (e.g., nodes 114, 469, and 769) are less important. The concepts of geographical center and periphery do not exist in graph theory; only the concept of connectivity does. In other words, nodes that exhibit better connectivity or play a crucial role in the networkâ€™s connectivity were trained with higher weights. Placing sensor nodes at critical connectivity points appears to be key to improving the performance of graph-based data-driven models. The importance related to node connectivity underscores the need to optimize sensor layouts, especially in selecting critical connections. However, further cross-network studies are needed to definitively establish the correlation between sensor placement and connectivity.

By introducing the self-attention mechanism into the gating mechanism, this study broadens the receptive field of sensor nodes and effectively captures global information. Additionally, it deepens our understanding of the working mechanisms of complex network prediction models. These findings provide theoretical support for optimizing existing models and lay a solid foundation for developing new models for future urban infrastructure monitoring and management.

## 5. Conclusions

This study proposes a novel multi-step demand forecasting solution for cities lacking hydraulic model support. By integrating forecasting and interpretability into a single, cohesive framework, it provides accurate and transparent predictions. Compared to other forecasting methods not reliant on hydraulic models, this study demonstrates the superiority of the proposed AGN method in both spatial generalization performance and its long-term accuracy in temporal forecasting performance. The high accuracy achieved further validates the feasibility of predicting water demand states from pressure measurements, offering a novel approach to demand forecasting. To the best of our knowledge, this work represents a pioneering effort to address interpretability within complex WDS forecasting models. By interpreting the model, this study gained deeper insights of the internal working mechanisms of complex network prediction models. Additionally, the interpretability framework aids model refinement, improving alignment with actual WDS behavior and enhancing overall predictive and interpretative performance.

Our open-source model lays a valuable foundation for developing machine learning-based models as an alternative to hydraulic models, which future research can further optimize. Future research should focus on enhancing the architecture of GNN and applying more interpretability methods to improve both performance and clarity. Model Performance: First, the model should be tested on more real-world WDSs to verify its robustness and explore the relationship between network characteristics and optimal parameter settings. Additionally, more water network features should be extracted, and various types of sensor data should be integrated to achieve multi-source information integration, enhancing the modelâ€™s alignment with practical problems. Furthermore, incorporating physical laws can constrain the predictions, improving both the accuracy and credibility of the model. Model Interpretability: The transparency of the model directly affects its deplorability in the water distribution field, which is characterized by robustness and safety. This study primarily provides model-level explanations. However, for complex model architectures, global explanations may be difficult to understand in depth. To fully comprehend the modelâ€™s outputs, future research should develop instance-level explanations that align better with human cognitive habits and are easier for non-technical users to understand. Examples of such methods include perturbation-based, gradient/feature-based, and decomposition-based approaches.

However, short-term demand forecasting in real-life WDSs proves challenging due to the need for predictions at all nodes based on limited sensor layouts and complex network topology. The stochastic variability of water usage, driven by human activities, complicates the forecasting process [26]. Given the hurdles in distribution reliability and the capricious nature of water consumption, accurate real-time demand forecasting is crucial for ensuring a reliable and resilient WDS [12].\\n45-\\n46:Generally, to estimate the water usage at non-monitored sites, a well-calibrated hydraulic model is typically required for indirect observation [4,5,9,15]. The hydraulic model, with their robust theoretical basis and comprehensible parameter settings, serves as the foundation and primary source of information for water network management [8,27,36], and is widely understood and trusted by practitioners. Hydraulic simulation was conducted to enhance the estimation credibility of a real-world network, and demand forecasting can then be obtained by time series forecasting algorithms [23]. However, the uncertainties associated with the water demand and the condition of infrastructure assets make constructing and maintaining a well-calibrated WDS highly complex and time-consuming [1,33]. Achieving real-time efficient forecasting based on the hydraulic model seems unattainable, especially for cities lacking a hydraulic model. Their research demonstrated the robustness of the GNN model to various levels of noise. By applying the gated graph neural network (GGNN), Li et al. [18] were the first to achieve the state forecasting for water quality. The GGNN algorithm, derived from gated recurrent unit (GRU), was applied for the first time in the domain of the WDS, yielding promising spatiotemporal predictive outputs. Although the aforementioned methods can be used for predicting future water demand states, they still have limitations. The methods in the first two studies were limited to estimating the current state. While integrating time series prediction algorithms with their methods could achieve future state forecasting, the simple stacking of time series forecasting and state estimation would lead to error accumulation. The GGNN method offers a novel approach to developing alternative models for state forecasting in WDSs. However, directly applying the GGNN method does not tightly integrate with the characteristics of water networks. Additionally, GGNN requires recurrent graph convolution operations to expand the receptive field, which limits its application in large-scale pipe networks and leads to over-smoothing issues.\\n47-\\n48-Despite excellent results and a broad range of applications, GNN models are still treated as black boxes [16]: the underlying mechanisms that steer the learning process and generalization ability remain a challenging and open question [29]. Merely achieving good performance is not enough to guarantee the deployment of models in production [13,21], especially in critical operational contexts where additional performance metrics such as robustness or safety are paramount. In such environments, human operators must comprehend and trust the model's predictions. Also, a deeper understanding of the model would improve its predictive performance [22]. Therefore, the second goal of this paper is to explain the results generated by the model.\\n49-\\n50-The literature [34] defines a classification standard for interpretability methods within the GNN framework. These methods are categorized into model-level and instance-level explanations. The former elucidates the behavior and decision-making process of the entire model, while the latter provides input-specific explanations for each graph instance. Model-based explanations offer a macroscopic understanding of the overall model behavior by identifying general patterns and rules under different input scenarios. Within this context, the concept of explainability is tied to specific model fragments. These explanations delve into the inference-making process of the model, providing a comprehensive account of which features significantly impact the inference results [14,28]. These techniques analyze the inference process to determine which features significantly influence outputs, enabling retrospective examination of parameter contributions. Model-level explanations can be further divided into three parts: rule extraction, hidden semantics analysis, and attribution methods [20].\\n51-\\n--\\n72-Fig. 1. The workflow of the proposed AGN model.\\n73-\\n74-(1) Neighbor propagation based on adjacency matrix: By multiplying candidate hidden pressure states (CHPS) 𝑐(𝑙−1)𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗 with the weighted adjacency matrix 𝒲𝑣𝑖, the past CHPS of sensor node 𝑣𝑖 is transferred to its first-order neighborhood nodes:((5))̅𝑐(𝑙−1)𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=𝒲𝑣𝑖·𝑐(𝑙−1)𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗 where 𝑙 is the iteration of recurrent layers, and the initial CHPS 𝑐(0)𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗 is an initialized learnable parameter.\\n75-## 3. Experimental setups\\n76-\\n77:### 3.1. Dataset\\n78-\\n79-Real-world WDSs typically lack comprehensive nodal monitoring, preventing direct validation of demand forecasts at non-instrumented nodes. To address this limitation and assess model performance under realistic conditions, this study adopt the benchmark l-Town Network dataset [30]. As depicted in Fig. 2(a), this network has substantial elevation variations, with a maximum difference of 74.2 m, and is serviced by two water tanks. It includes notable high and low areas where water pressure is regulated by a pressurized pumping station and a pressure-reducing valve, respectively. This network essentially covers the characteristics found in typical real WDSs.\\n80-\\n81-Image: Fig 2\\n82-\\n83-  1. Download: Download high-res image (1MB)\\n84-  2. Download: Download full-size image\\n85-\\n86:Fig. 2. Nodal features of l-Town Network. (a) Nodal height features (b) Nodal type features (c) Variation of different water usage types over a year.\\n87-## 4. Results and discussion\\n88-\\n89-This section presents the results from both forecasting performance and model explainability analyses. First, this study report and analyze the forecasting task results: the proposed method achieves superior spatial generalization and temporal forecasting accuracy compared to previous approaches. Subsequently, this study evaluates the robustness of the AGN model across varying prediction windows. Finally, once demonstrated the quality of our predictions, this study explains the recurrent layers, attention mechanisms and nodal features used in the model, demonstrating how these components influence the final output of the model.\\n90-\\n91-### 4.1. Forecasting results\\n--\\n149-\\n150-Z. Cai, N. [14]\\n214-\\n--\\n223-\\n224-2025, Results in Engineering\\n225-\\n226-Show abstract\\n227-\\n228:Haze pollution, driven by PM_{2.5}, poses significant risks to public health and environmental sustainability, particularly in urban-industrial regions like the Fenwei Plain, China. This study introduces the Lasso-Cox Dynamic Attention Network (LCDAN), a novel multimodal framework for modeling PM_{2.5} transmission risks. By integrating Generalized Autoregressive Conditional Heteroskedasticity (GARCH (1,1)) for volatility analysis, graph neural networks (GNNs) with attention mechanisms for spatial path representation, extended Long Short-Term Memory (xLSTM) for temporal dynamics, and SHAP for interpretability, LCDAN captures the complex spatio-temporal evolution of PM_{2.5} propagation networks. Using a comprehensive dataset (2019–2023) encompassing air pollutants, meteorological, socio-economic, and geographic data, the model outperforms eight benchmarks, achieving a 5.5% average F1 score improvement and up to 38% for low-risk scenarios. A case study in Xi’an (November–December 2023) demonstrates LCDAN’s ability to identify critical transmission pathways and risk nodes, with SHAP analysis highlighting industrial emissions and economic density as key drivers. The model’s efficiency (42-minute training, <15 ms online inference) and interpretability make it a robust tool for air quality management, offering actionable insights for policymakers to mitigate haze pollution effectively.\\n229-\\n230-  * ### Diagnosing the Roles of Meteorological Forcing, Storage Memory, and Network Connectivity in Cold-Region Streamflow Prediction\\n231-\\n232-2026, Water Resources Management\\n233-\\n--\\n245-\\n246-Edited by Dr. Komali Kantamaneni (University of Central Lancashire, Preston, , United Kingdom), Professor Upaka Rathnayake (Atlantic Technological University - Sligo, Sligo, , Ireland), Assist. Results and discussion\\r\\n\\r\\nThis section presents the results from both forecasting performance and model explainability analyses. First, this study report and analyze the forecasting task results: the proposed method achieves superior spatial generalization and temporal forecasting accuracy compared to previous approaches. Subsequently, this study evaluates the robustness of the AGN model across varying prediction windows. Finally, once demonstrated the quality of our predictions, this study explains the recurrent layers, attention mechanisms and nodal features used in the model, demonstrating how these components influence the final output of the model.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 4.1. Forecasting results\\r\\n\\r\\n#### 4.1.1. Spatial generalization accuracy\\r\\n\\r\\nThis study aims to utilize the GNN algorithm to predict the water demand states from a pressure perspective. A thorough review of previous research revealed that, although several pioneering studies have explored the estimation or forecasting of WDS states without relying on pre-built hydraulic models, they still face limitations. Notably, 82.8 % of total nodes have an MAE below 0.5 m, a widely recognized threshold for practical application in hydraulic model calibration. Compared to competing models, AGN shows a significant advantage in the proportion of nodes below this threshold, outperforming GGNN and GHR+GRU methods by 79 % and 73.4 %, respectively, highlighting its superior spatial generalization performance.\\r\\n\\r\\nFurther analysis of Fig. 3(b) reveals a profound correlation between prediction error and nodal head variation. Nodes with errors between 0.5 m and 1.0 m are mainly concentrated in high-elevation areas, while low-elevation nodes generally face challenges with MAE greater than 2.0 m. This phenomenon indicates an imbalance in model training, particularly as low-elevation nodes are not sufficiently learned due to sparse sensor placement. We attribute this primarily to two factors: (1) sensor scarcity in low-elevation regions hinders the model's ability to capture localized hydraulic characteristics, and (2) insufficient model capacity to discern elevation-driven hydraulic variations. These findings underscore the critical impact of sensor deployment density on data-driven model performance, highlighting the need for comprehensive data acquisition to optimize model training.\\r\\n\\r\\nImage: Fig 3\\r\\n\\r\\n  1. Download: Download high-res image (1MB)\\r\\n  2. Download: Download full-size image\\r\\n\\r\\nFig. 3. Comparison of forecasting models in spatial direction: (a) The real nodal heads variation; (b) The MAE loss of AGN model; (c) The MAE loss of GGNN model; (d) The MAE loss of GHR+GRU method.\\r\\n\\r\\nAdmittedly, in the face of complex water network components, drastic elevation changes, and sparse sensor deployment, the AGN model still faces considerable challenges in pursuing comprehensive and accurate predictions. However, it is noteworthy that even under such extreme conditions, the AGN model demonstrates remarkable predictive ability in the high-elevation area, despite the sensors in this area still being relatively sparse â€” 100 % of high-elevation nodes have an MAE below 1.0 m. In the context of low-density sensor deployment, the AGN can still effectively address sparse sensor environments, reducing reliance on sensing equipment. More real-world WDSs will benefit from our sensor-robust model.\\r\\n\\r\\nAs shown in Fig. 3(b), the AGN model achieves satisfactory prediction accuracy and breadth in the medium-elevation area. This suggests that in water distribution systems (WDSs) characterized by relatively gentle terrain and moderate head changes, the AGN model can deliver enhanced predictive performance. These results not only further consolidate the practicality and reliability of the AGN model but also lays a solid theoretical and practical foundation for its widespread application in complex WDS management, heralding a promising future for data-driven technology in intelligent WDS management.\\r\\n\\r\\nDue to their relatively low deployment and maintenance costs, pressure sensors have been widely used in WDSs. This provides a rich data source for the AGN model and practically verifies the applicability and feasibility of water demand prediction from pressure data. This practice-oriented research perspective offers new ideas and methodological support for WDS management.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nFurther comparative analysis reveals that, unlike AGN and GGNN which also incorporate GRU components, the GHR+GRU method exhibits significant instability in its time-series performance, with markedly increased fluctuation amplitudes. This phenomenon may indicate the limitations of the GHR method in handling water networks with extreme head variations and suboptimal sensor placement. It emphasizes the spatial generalization challenges of accurately predicting non-sensor nodesâ€™ state based on limited sensor measurements. And this is the key challenge in data-driven water network modeling.\\r\\n\\r\\nAlthough this study achieves high-precision predictions with MAPE not exceeding 5 % over time, it seems unacceptable when faced with a wide head variation range of 41.1 m to 113.6 m (Fig. 3(a)). This is mainly due to the approximately 4 % of low-elevation nodes, whose MAPE reaches as high as 80 %, significantly raising the overall average MAPE. The error levels for different elevation regions are analyzed in detail in the next section.\\r\\nThis is primarily due to the attention mechanismâ€™s ability to capture long-distance correlations, allowing nodes to transcend physical distance limitations. Through the self-attention mechanism, the model can learn and transfer the pressure characteristics from sensor nodes to all nodes, rather than being limited to the sensor nodesâ€™ neighborhood. Therefore, spatial generalizing process can be summarized as two significant mechanisms: neighbor topology feature transferring based on graph convolution, and global feature generalization based on the self-attention mechanism. Based on these two key mechanisms, the AGN model can generalize the learned graph structure-related features to all nodes in WDS, achieving state forecasting.\\r\\n\\r\\nIn the GGNN, ensuring sensor nodes can â€œperceiveâ€ the farthest non-sensor nodes theoretically requires up to 11 steps of recurrent iterations. However, this approach presents significant practical challenges, particularly due to the substantial increase in training time. For example, …4622 tokens truncated…, E. ### aggregated_output

```text
44-As urban populations and city sizes continue to grow, urban water distribution systems (WDSs), as a fundamental component for ensuring public welfare, are facing increasing complexity and management challenges [10,25]. To maintain the stability of WDSs, accurate forecasting and proactive preparation for future demands are essential [11,35]. The predictive lead time establishes critical temporal buffers for municipal water operators, strengthening their operational resilience. However, short-term demand forecasting in real-life WDSs proves challenging due to the need for predictions at all nodes based on limited sensor layouts and complex network topology. The stochastic variability of water usage, driven by human activities, complicates the forecasting process [26]. Given the hurdles in distribution reliability and the capricious nature of water consumption, accurate real-time demand forecasting is crucial for ensuring a reliable and resilient WDS [12].
45-
46:Generally, to estimate the water usage at non-monitored sites, a well-calibrated hydraulic model is typically required for indirect observation [4,5,9,15]. The hydraulic model, with their robust theoretical basis and comprehensible parameter settings, serves as the foundation and primary source of information for water network management [8,27,36], and is widely understood and trusted by practitioners. Hydraulic simulation was conducted to enhance the estimation credibility of a real-world network, and demand forecasting can then be obtained by time series forecasting algorithms [23]. However, the uncertainties associated with the water demand and the condition of infrastructure assets make constructing and maintaining a well-calibrated WDS highly complex and time-consuming [1,33]. Achieving real-time efficient forecasting based on the hydraulic model seems unattainable, especially for cities lacking a hydraulic model. Consequently, the primary objective of this study focuses on developing an innovative state estimation framework that operates independently of conventional hydraulic modeling paradigms.
47-
48-Several studies [18,33,37] have enabled direct modeling of WDSs, obviating the need for hydraulic models. Based on the assumption of smooth variations in nodal head (the sum of nodal pressure and elevation of a node), Zhou et al. [37] developed a graph-based head reconstruction (GHR) method. This method decomposes the hydraulic head into components with different variation speeds, reconstructing the head using the slowly varying components obtained from a limited number of monitoring nodes. However, this method heavily relies on having a sufficient number of well-defined sensor nodes and a regular underlying graph structure. Xing and Sela [33] were the pioneers in employing a graph neural network (GNN) to achieve state estimation within WDSs. They developed an encoder-decoder GNN architecture, enabling real-time synchronous estimation of pipeline flow and nodal pressure states. Their research demonstrated the robustness of the GNN model to various levels of noise. By applying the gated graph neural network (GGNN), Li et al. [18] were the first to achieve the state forecasting for water quality. The GGNN algorithm, derived from gated recurrent unit (GRU), was applied for the first time in the domain of the WDS, yielding promising spatiotemporal predictive outputs. Although the aforementioned methods can be used for predicting future water demand states, they still have limitations. The methods in the first two studies were limited to estimating the current state. While integrating time series prediction algorithms with their methods could achieve future state forecasting, the simple stacking of time series forecasting and state estimation would lead to error accumulation. The GGNN method offers a novel approach to developing alternative models for state forecasting in WDSs. However, directly applying the GGNN method does not tightly integrate with the characteristics of water networks. Additionally, GGNN requires recurrent graph convolution operations to expand the receptive field, which limits its application in large-scale pipe networks and leads to over-smoothing issues.
--
132-This work was supported by the National Key Research and Development Program of China (2023YFC3208102, 2022YFF0606905), and the Ningbo science and technology plan project (2023Z057).
133-
134:## Data availability
135-
136-Data will be made available on request.
--
236-Explainable artificial intelligence for sustainable urban water systems engineering
237-
238:Results Eng., 25 (2025), Article 104349, 10.1016/j.rineng.2025.104349
239-
240-View PDFView articleView in ScopusGoogle Scholar
32-
33:The l-Town benchmark network comprises 50 predefined residential and commercial water demand patterns, along with 4 industrial demand patterns. To enhance the model’s relevance to real-world scenarios, this study slightly modified the l-Town network. Specifically, this study randomly assigned these 104 demand patterns to the 782 demand nodes. Fig. 2(b) depicts the water usage type at each demand node; Fig. 2(c) illustrates all water demand pattern curves, with grey lines representing individual patterns and the red line indicating their mean values. A total of one year of sequence data with a time step of 5 min was generated. The training, validation, and testing datasets were constructed by chronologically assigning 70 %, 20 %, and 10 % of the total samples, respectively.
34-

Given the efficiency of the data-driven approach and the intricate node-line structure of water networks, graph-based machine learning methods offer a promising approach. However, the scarcity of real-time flow acquisition devices limits the ability to directly forecast timely demand. Pressure sensors provide a more practical alternative. Thus, this study proposes a novel attention-augmented gated graph neural network (AGN) for real-time demand forecasting from a pressure perspective. By integrating attention mechanisms, the AGN overcomes limitations of convolution-based graph neural networks, such as capturing long-range dependencies and dynamic node interactions, leading to enhanced performance on real-world modeling problems. This model is formulated for real and sensitive contexts where, in addition to being accurate, the predictions must also be understandable by human operators. Thus, model-level explanations are then introduced in our explainable framework. The proposed AGN outperforms benchmark methods in both spatiotemporal accuracy and long-term forecasting effectiveness, while simultaneously delivering interpretable insights into the demand forecasting process. The novel approach based on the fusion of forecasting and explainability in a single framework enables the creation of powerful and reliable systems suitable for real-world issues and challenges.\n\n\nZekun Z. et al. ### 2.1. Problem formulation

Before discussing the issues and challenges, this study provide a detailed introduction to the symbolic representation of WDS as a graph. The WDS is represented as an undirected graph ð’¢=(ð’±,â„°,ð’²). Building upon the dynamic global weighting capability of attention mechanisms, this study enhances the GGNN framework by integrating self-attention and external-attention modules. The self-attention module addresses the limited ability of standard graph convolutions to capture long-range spatial dependencies among nodes, significantly improving spatial representational capacity. The external-attention module explicitly models the relationships between nodal attributes and pressure observations, enhancing the model's physical relevance to WDSs. ## 3. Experimental setups

### 3.1. Dataset

Real-world WDSs typically lack comprehensive nodal monitoring, preventing direct validation of demand forecasts at non-instrumented nodes. To address this limitation and assess model performance under realistic conditions, this study adopt the benchmark l-Town Network dataset [30]. As depicted in Fig. 2(a), this network has substantial elevation variations, with a maximum difference of 74.2 m, and is serviced by two water tanks. It includes notable high and low areas where water pressure is regulated by a pressurized pumping station and a pressure-reducing valve, respectively. This network essentially covers the characteristics found in typical real WDSs.

Image: Fig 2

  1. Download: Download high-res image (1MB)
  2. Download: Download full-size image

Fig. 2. Nodal features of l-Town Network. (a) Nodal height features (b) Nodal type features (c) Variation of different water usage types over a year.

The l-Town benchmark network comprises 50 predefined residential and commercial water demand patterns, along with 4 industrial demand patterns. To enhance the modelâ€™s relevance to real-world scenarios, this study slightly modified the l-Town network. Specifically, this study randomly assigned these 104 demand patterns to the 782 demand nodes. Fig. 2(b) depicts the water usage type at each demand node; Fig. 2(c) illustrates all water demand pattern curves, with grey lines representing individual patterns and the red line indicating their mean values. A total of one year of sequence data with a time step of 5 min was generated. The training, validation, and testing datasets were constructed by chronologically assigning 70 %, 20 %, and 10 % of the total samples, respectively.

The pressurized pumping station that provides additional pressure to the high zone is represented as a node with known pressures for prediction. Its nodal pressures were obtained from pump characteristic curves and flow sensors installed on the pump. In the weighted adjacency matrix, the two pipelines connected to the â€œpumping station nodeâ€ are represented by a sufficiently large number (300 in this example), while the pressure-reducing valve is represented by a sufficiently small number (1 in this example).

### 3.2. Model settings

For the forecasting tasks, this study adopted the GHR proposed by Zhou et al. [37] and the GGNN used by Li et al. [18] to effectively compare our proposed AGN model with previous studies. Especially, this study combined the GHRâ€™s state estimation results with the GRU time prediction algorithm (GHR+GRU) to achieve forecasting task and extended the GGNN from water quality to the task of nodal demand.

For all three models (AGN, GGNN, and GRU), the hidden layer dimension was set to 12. All three neural networks were trained using the ADAM optimizer with a training batch size of 32. The initial learning rate was set to 1e-3, and it was reduced by a factor of 0.8 after 1 training epoch. If the training error does not decrease over 10 batches, the epoch will be terminated early. For the AGN and GGNN, the number of recurrent layers was set to 3. The forecasting step length is set to be equal to the entered historical step length.

### 3.3. Evaluation metric

This study reported the Mean Absolute Error (MAE) and Mean Absolute Percentage Error (MAPE) of forecasting results for all 783 nodes (including 1 â€œpumping station nodeâ€) in different forecasting time steps, to evaluate the model's spatial generalization and time series forecasting effect. The formula is as follows:(13)ð‘€â¢ð´â¢ð¸=1(ð¼+ð¼*)Ã—ð½*â¢ð¼+ð¼*âˆ‘ð‘–=1ð½*âˆ‘ð‘—=1âˆ£â„Žð‘£ð‘–,ð‘¡ð‘—âˆ’Ìƒâ„Žð‘£ð‘–,ð‘¡ð‘—âˆ£(14)ð‘€â¢ð´â¢ð‘ƒâ¢ð¸=1(ð¼+ð¼*)Ã—ð½*â¢ð¼+ð¼*âˆ‘ð‘–=1ð½*âˆ‘ð‘—=1âˆ£â„Žð‘£ð‘–,ð‘¡ð‘—âˆ’Ìƒâ„Žð‘£ð‘–,ð‘¡ð‘—âˆ£â„Žð‘£ð‘–,ð‘¡ð‘— where â„Žð‘£ð‘–,ð‘¡ð‘— is the prediction and Ìƒâ„Žð‘£ð‘–,ð‘¡ð‘— is the simulated measurement of vector ð‘£ð‘– at time step ð‘¡ð‘—.

Given the efficiency of the data-driven approach and the intricate node-line structure of water networks, graph-based machine learning methods offer a promising approach. However, the scarcity of real-time flow acquisition devices limits the ability to directly forecast timely demand. Pressure sensors provide a more practical alternative. Thus, this study proposes a novel attention-augmented gated graph neural network (AGN) for real-time demand forecasting from a pressure perspective. By integrating attention mechanisms, the AGN overcomes limitations of convolution-based graph neural networks, such as capturing long-range dependencies and dynamic node interactions, leading to enhanced performance on real-world modeling problems. This model is formulated for real and sensitive contexts where, in addition to being accurate, the predictions must also be understandable by human operators. Introduction\n\nAs urban populations and city sizes continue to grow, urban water distribution systems (WDSs), as a fundamental component for ensuring public welfare, are facing increasing complexity and management challenges [10,25]. To maintain the stability of WDSs, accurate forecasting and proactive preparation for future demands are essential [11,35]. The predictive lead time establishes critical temporal buffers for municipal water operators, strengthening their operational resilience. However, short-term demand forecasting in real-life WDSs proves challenging due to the need for predictions at all nodes based on limited sensor layouts and complex network topology. However, directly applying the GGNN method does not tightly integrate with the characteristics of water networks. Additionally, GGNN requires recurrent graph convolution operations to expand the receptive field, which limits its application in large-scale pipe networks and leads to over-smoothing issues.\n\nDespite excellent results and a broad range of applications, GNN models are still treated as black boxes [16]: the underlying mechanisms that steer the learning process and generalization ability remain a challenging and open question [29]. Merely achieving good performance is not enough to guarantee the deployment of models in production [13,21], especially in critical operational contexts where additional performance metrics such as robustness or safety are paramount. In such environments, human operators must comprehend and trust the model's predictions. Also, a deeper understanding of the model would improve its predictive performance [22]. Therefore, the second goal of this paper is to explain the results generated by the model.\n\nThis study innovative proposes an explainable attention-augmented gated graph neural network (AGN) framework for water demand states forecasting from a pressure perspective. Derived from GRU-based GNN (i.e. GGNN), this model not only exhibits excellent time series prediction performance but also captures the network’s structure. The innovative attention mechanisms are then incorporated to address the limitation of GGNN in capturing solely local dependencies and enhance the model's interpretability through its transparent decision-making process. Overall, the paper encompasses the following novelties:\n\n·Demand forecasting model designed for water network characteristics: A core innovation of this study is the development of the AGN model, specifically designed to capture the unique spatio-temporal characteristics of water distribution networks. The proposed AGN model forecasts future demands at all nodes, without relying on hydraulic models. The AGN model shows significant accuracy in both spatial and temporal dimensions compared to existing direct modeling methods. The model maintains high predictive robustness across various forecasting horizons. Our high accuracy results validate the feasibility and efficiency of demand prediction from a pressure perspective.\n\nModel explanation associated with water network characteristics: Merely achieving sufficient prediction accuracy is not enough to guarantee the model’s deployment in real-world WDSs. To bridge this gap, the second innovation addresses the crucial need for improved model transparency. Specifically, the key lies in integrating model explanations with practical engineering issues, enabling network managers to understand and trust the model, thereby making reliable decisions. To the best knowledge, we are the first to address interpretability issues in WDS. By providing these explanations, this study provided a deeper understanding of the roles of different model components in the prediction task. This process also aids in improving the model by offering feedback to further enhance its predictive performance.\n\n## 2. Methodology\n\nThis study proposes an Attentive Graph Network (AGN) to forecast water demand in distribution systems using pressure data without hydraulic modeling. The architecture leverages the inherent graph structure of water networks, historical pressure measurements, and nodal features to predict future nodal demands. Specifically designed to capture unique pressure characteristics in water distribution systems (WDS), the model includes built-in interpretability mechanisms to ensure transparency. Results and discussion\n\nThis section presents the results from both forecasting performance and model explainability analyses. Forecasting results\n\n#### 4.1.1. Spatial generalization accuracy\n\nThis study aims to utilize the GNN algorithm to predict the water demand states from a pressure perspective. A thorough review of previous research revealed that, although several pioneering studies have explored the estimation or forecasting of WDS states without relying on pre-built hydraulic models, they still face limitations. \nDespite these limitations, the results of the demand prediction displayed along the time axis clearly show the long-term robustness of the AGN model in practical deployment environments. Notably, the model was trained on a consumer-grade GPU platform (NVIDIA GeForce RTX 4060 Ti), requiring approximately 3 h in total. Leveraging the computational efficiency inherent to deep learning methods, the model achieves an inference time of <1 s per run, sufficiently satisfies the requirement for real-time prediction. These findings not only enhance our confidence in the model’s performance but also provides strong support and insights for its future application in broader and more complex scenarios. Subsequent sections analyze prediction accuracy variations across time steps and regions.\n\n#### 4.1.3. Accuracy analysis at varying time steps\n\nTo thoroughly investigate the prediction accuracy of the proposed AGN model across three different elevation regions with varying prediction steps, we tested four different prediction steps and presented the MAE and MAPE for the low, medium, and high regions separately (Table 1). ## 5. Conclusions\n\nThis study proposes a novel multi-step demand forecasting solution for cities lacking hydraulic model support. By integrating forecasting and interpretability into a single, cohesive framework, it provides accurate and transparent predictions. Compared to other forecasting methods not reliant on hydraulic models, this study demonstrates the superiority of the proposed AGN method in both spatial generalization performance and its long-term accuracy in temporal forecasting performance. The high accuracy achieved further validates the feasibility of predicting water demand states from pressure measurements, offering a novel approach to demand forecasting. To the best of our knowledge, this work represents a pioneering effort to address interpretability within complex WDS forecasting models. By interpreting the model, this study gained deeper insights of the internal working mechanisms of complex network prediction models. Additionally, the interpretability framework aids model refinement, improving alignment with actual WDS behavior and enhancing overall predictive and interpretative performance.\n\nIn a sustainable and cost-effective WDS, this research offers a valuable tool for efficient demand modeling for numerous cities lacking hydraulic models. Our research findings have practical implications beyond the benchmark we considered: cities seeking to optimize water distribution and enhance the management level of WDSs will benefit from the insights provided by our framework. The findings hold practical significance extending beyond the specific case studies presented: Cities aiming to optimize water distribution and improve management practices will benefit from the insights offered by our framework. Water utilities can leverage the interpretable predictions to enhance confidence in their scheduling decisions.\n\nHowever, this study still has certain limitations and gaps in practical applications: (1) potential information leakage during the model tuning process when determining optimal parameters based on model performance; (2) uncertainties related to sensor acquisition errors and infrastructure conditions; and (3) the inability to satisfy all three conservation equations at the same time.\nThis study primarily provides model-level explanations. However, for complex model architectures, global explanations may be difficult to understand in depth. To fully comprehend the model’s outputs, future research should develop instance-level explanations that align better with human cognitive habits and are easier for non-technical users to understand. Examples of such methods include perturbation-based, gradient/feature-based, and decomposition-based approaches.\n\n## CRediT authorship contribution statement\n\nZekun Zou: Writing – review & editing, Writing – original draft, Software, Methodology, Investigation, Formal analysis, Conceptualization. Tanwar\n## Cited by (3)\n\n  * ### Spatio-temporal network risk evolution of PM2.5 transmission pathways considering volatility\n\n2025, Results in Engineering\n\nShow abstract\n\nHaze pollution, driven by PM_{2.5}, poses significant risks to public health and environmental sustainability, particularly in urban-industrial regions like the Fenwei Plain, China. This study introduces the Lasso-Cox Dynamic Attention Network (LCDAN), a novel multimodal framework for modeling PM_{2.5} transmission risks. By integrating Generalized Autoregressive Conditional Heteroskedasticity (GARCH (1,1)) for volatility analysis, graph neural networks (GNNs) with attention mechanisms for spatial path representation, extended Long Short-Term Memory (xLSTM) for temporal dynamics, and SHAP for interpretability, LCDAN captures the complex spatio-temporal evolution of PM_{2.5} propagation networks. Using a comprehensive dataset (2019–2023) encompassing air pollutants, meteorological, socio-economic, and geographic data, the model outperforms eight benchmarks, achieving a 5.5% average F1 score improvement and up to 38% for low-risk scenarios. A case study in Xi’an (November–December 2023) demonstrates LCDAN’s ability to identify critical transmission pathways and risk nodes, with SHAP analysis highlighting industrial emissions and economic density as key drivers. The model’s efficiency (42-minute training, <15 ms online inference) and interpretability make it a robust tool for air quality management, offering actionable insights for policymakers to mitigate haze pollution effectively.\n\n  * ### Enhanced Coordinated Sensor Network for Real-Time Lake Kivu Water Quality Monitoring\n\n2026, International Journal of Safety and Security Engineering\n\n  * ### A physics-informed AI methodology for Prognostics and Health Management of complex systems under low data constraints\n\n2026, Journal of Intelligent Manufacturing\n\n© 2025 The Authors.
